THE BUTTERFLY EFFECT

The machine stops screaming before you do.

You sit strapped in, sweating, your hands shaking on the armrests. It was a systems test. Nothing was
supposed to go anywhere. The panels are dark and the controls are dead.

You open the hatch expecting the concrete floor of the test bay. You get dirt. A mule cart rattles
past close enough to touch, and the driver shouts at you in Latin. You understand him.

Thank God I took that in college.

Ancient Rome. Not ruins. Alive.

The machine is dead weight. Its contacts and couplings are gold, and gold you can carry: you pry out
60 aurei worth of it. Every piece will have to go back before the machine could ever run again.
Rome pays in silver denarii. Gold has to be changed at the money changers (exchange <n> aurei).

Two things on this street could use your money, and you can afford only one:
  choose fountain  — The Subura's fountain runs brown. Clear the lead pipe and reline the basin.
  choose workshop  — Pay for a second forge and paid apprentices at the smith's by the Porta Trigemina, in exchange for a share of the business.
Your ears are still ringing. Before anything else you could walk and get your bearings: visit market, changers, forges, curia or subura (no Attention).
Type 'help' for commands.

  • At the small baths behind the Circus Maximus you sit in the warm room between a fuller and a man selling olives. The olive man complains that a merchant under the Aventine pays porters to bail his cellar every morning, and still the jars go green. The fuller says the merchant should sacrifice to the river and stop complaining.

== Month 6 · Iunius, AD 155 ==  732 denarii (+11/month) · 20 aurei (1 = 25 den.)   Index 100.3

  Machine: 0/9 repair steps, gold 0/60 aurei   (machine)

> commission look cellarpump
Cassianus's cellar smells of wet sacking. The pump is a good bronze force pump, two cylinders and a rocking beam, the kind the fire watch carries. The plungers are sound. Water spurts back past the leather flaps on every stroke: the seats are worn into ovals, and the last man who fixed them packed the gaps with wax. Cassianus watches you trace the leak with a finger. "Can you fix it, or do I buy another pump?"
"Here is what I'll do," he says. "I buy the bronze and the leather. You make it pump. Three hundred denarii now, five hundred when my cellar is dry." (Cassianus pays 300 denarii now, 500 denarii on completion; Cassianus buys the materials. Work: about 3 months, 1 Attention a month. commission accept cellarpump / counter cellarpump / decline cellarpump)  [Attention left this month: 3/4]

> commission accept cellarpump
Agreed. Cassianus pays 800 denarii in all (300 denarii now, 500 denarii on completion); Cassianus buys the materials. You receive 300 denarii now.  [Attention left this month: 2/4]

  • High summer. Anyone with money has gone to the hills. The streets stink by noon, the fountains run warm, and the physicians say the bad air rises off the Tiber marshes at dusk. You keep the shutters closed and work at night.

  • At the market a sausage seller shouts that his sausages are from Lucania and his prices are from heaven. A man with a basket of snails haggles with him over a trade and wins. You eat a sausage standing up, the Roman way, and burn your mouth.

Lollius's pumps are chain pumps and one bronze force pump, all clogged with sand and pitch. The force pump has the same oval seats as Cassianus's. The chain pumps are a different trouble: the buckets jam where the chain bends. Lollius stands over you the whole time and talks about the price of grain in Alexandria.
"I pay for the bronze," Lollius says. "Four hundred now, eight hundred and fifty when all three ships go a week without bailing. And if they leak after, I tell every shipper on the river." (Lollius pays 405 denarii now, 862 denarii on completion; Lollius buys the materials. A boy is relieving himself against the next tomb along.

Prima asks where your household gods are. Felix answers for you: in a box, travelling. Everyone laughs, and Prima keeps looking at you.

  • The year AD 161 begins.
  • Another year as a member of the Merchants' Guild of Ostia: your seniority raises your stake to 13%.
  • Gaius has you to dinner above the shop: lentils, sausage, a jug of Sabine wine. His wife Livia has a green ribbon in her hair for the races and asks whether foreigners are allowed to have opinions about chariots. When you say the Blues looked strong she tells Gaius to stop inviting you. She refills your cup anyway.

The millwright, Aulus Septimius Crispus, is up to his knees in the tail race clearing mud. He looks at your drawing of one wheel driving four machines. "Where does a man stand to grease that one?" he says, and goes back to the mud.

  • Rome already grinds its grain by water; the mills on the Janiculum turn all day. What no one has done is put several different machines on one wheel and keep them running with parts any millwright can cut and change. Grand Challenge: Powered workshops. Can one power source reliably drive several useful workshop machines, with components and maintenance Roman craftsmen can reproduce? (challenge power)

  • Aulus walks you down the race. Here the water comes smooth; there it spills over the boards and the wheel stutters. The mill with the clever extra gears is the one that fouls with mud every spring. He picks a wheel below the third mill: an old one, easy water, an owner who owes him money.
  • Recruiters are in the Forum: the war with Parthia needs men. A centurion with a vine stick looks over the young men who come forward, checks their teeth like a horse dealer's, and turns away half. A boy from your insula is taken; his mother walks beside him as far as the Porta Capena.

You show him how to keep it in a wax book instead, one line a day. He says a man who writes things down is a clerk. The next morning the book is full.

  • A long oak shaft on bronze shells that sit in split blocks, so a worn shell can be knocked out and another dropped in. Aulus has Gaius cut the shells to your gauge, six alike. "Grease this before the wheel starts. Every morning," Aulus says. "Or buy another bearing."
  • Old Lucan, who taught Gaius, watches you explain the shaft at the tavern with a finger in spilled wine. When you finish he turns your cup upside down. "Do not become a man who only has ideas," he says. "Come to my forge tomorrow. I'll show you why your cutters will break." They break exactly where he said.

  • Gaius's lathe goes on the shaft first, belted from a pulley, and turns bearing shells for the next block. He stands back from it with his arms folded. When the first shell comes off true he picks it up, turns it over, and puts it down without a word.
  • Up at the mills before dawn, you hear the wheel start, and at the same moment, back at the workshop, the machine's panel wakes on its own. The log shows a short burst of signal and an answer: the same pattern as the last second before the crash, a request and an acceptance. Nothing in Rome is sending anything.

A rope drive with a lever to slacken it lets one machine stop while the shaft turns on. "Move that bench," Aulus says. "Someone will reach across the shaft for a hammer." Then: "Tie your sleeves."
  • The grain fleet comes in all at once after a month of nothing: two hundred ships in the harbor at Ostia, and every barge on the river busy. Lollius's men work three days without sleeping.

  • A courier from the East rides in through the Porta Flaminia, and by evening the whole city knows: Armenia retaken. The baths are full of men who have never been east of Ostia explaining the campaign.

  • A boring bar on the second pulley, held rigid between two blocks, and a grinding wheel on the third. The bar bores a cylinder straighter than any hand. The wheel takes the rough off castings that Gaius's men used to file for a day. His men watch it the way men watch a horse they haven't decided about.
  • The cobbler's wife has left him for a sausage maker from the next street. The cobbler sings sadder songs and makes better shoes.

  • Aulus's back gives out lifting a stone that his sons told him to leave. He is on a board in his kitchen for a season, giving orders through the window. His younger son keeps the mills and does everything slightly differently, on purpose.
  • You show Aulus a drawing with a gear train to change the speed of the grinder. He looks at it for a long time. "Mud," he says. "Every one of those teeth fills with mud in March, and the man who cleans them loses a finger in April. Use a bigger pulley." You use a bigger pulley.

  (No Attention left this month. Type 'end' to end the month.)

> end
  • Three months of real orders on the wheel. The tally book fills: a shell changed in the second week, a rope that stretched, a day lost to a flood in the race and a day to a festival. By the end Aulus's sons at Ostia ask for the drawings, and a millwright Aulus never trained builds a shaft from them that runs.
  • One wheel drives a lathe, a boring bar and a grinder, and the parts that wear can be cut and changed by men who never met you. Nobody calls it anything. The millwrights call it Aulus's shaft.
  • A finishing-shop owner, Sextus Nerius, comes to watch the grinder. Twelve men polish and fit bronze in his shop by hand, some of them for twenty years. He watches the wheel take the rough off a casting in the time it takes one of his men to find his file. He doesn't say anything. He comes back the next day and watches again.

== Month 106 · October, AD 163 ==  7,568 denarii (+3/month) · 20 aurei (1 = 28.2 den.)   Index 104.1
  Attention: 4 free / 4 total

  ► Sextus Nerius's twelve men. Sextus Nerius comes to you at Gaius's shop with his hat in his hands, which he has never done before. "If I buy one of these wheels," he says, "what happens to my men?"
      decide change   say the machines change the work more than they end it
      decide honest   admit some of his men may lose their work
      decide workers  tell him to ask his men
      decide motive   ask whether he wants more work or lower wages
      decide inspect  go and see which of his tasks the wheel would change first
  The Guild (Publius Aelius Sabinus): you hold 15% (influence), strength 40 (44.4% of Economy)

That challenge is over.

> repair coil
The coil is finished.

> repair coolant
The coolant is finished.

> repair chronometer
The chronometer is finished.

> decide 1
Decide change (say the machines change the work more than they end it), honest (admit some of his men may lose their work), workers (tell him to ask his men), motive (ask whether he wants more work or lower wages), inspect (go and see which of his tasks the wheel would change first).

> decide workers
He asks them. The oldest says he is too old to learn a wheel. Two of the young ones ask to be taught to run it. Sextus puts off buying for a year, and when he buys, the two young men run it and the old one teaches them to judge a finish by eye.

> take ship
Take one of: ironwork.

> take tools
Take one of: ironwork.

> take instruments
Take one of: ironwork.

> work odd
You earn 146 denarii (13 denarii of it through your 1 membership); tax takes 15 denarii, you keep 132 denarii.  [Attention left this month: 3/4]

> end
  • Aulus is back on the race, slower, with a stick he pretends is for poking the sluices.
  • Lucan's eyes have gone. He sits on a stool by the forge he gave to his best apprentice and listens to the hammering. "Too cold," he says, without looking. The apprentice puts the iron back in the fire.

  • You pass Sextus Nerius's shop on the Clivus. Through the door you can hear the grinder and, under it, men talking as they work. One of the old finishers is teaching a boy to judge a polish by the way it holds the light. Sextus sees you and nods, and doesn't come out.

  • The year AD 165 begins.
  • Another year as a member of the Merchants' Guild of Ostia: your seniority raises your stake to 17%.
  • Winter again. The streets are quiet after dark; the lamplighters don't bother with the alleys. You go home by the main streets with a hand on your knife, the way everyone does.

  • For the Parentalia Livia takes bread soaked in wine and a few violets out to her parents' tomb on the Via Appia, and you carry the basket. She pours the wine through a pipe into the ground so they can drink it. On the way back she is quieter than you have ever heard her, and then she isn't.

The audience knows every joke before it comes and laughs harder for it. Afterward a mime does a drunk senator and nobody laughs at all, and the mime is very pleased.

> people
  Felix, a freedman who fits out ships at Ostia and in Rome, and a member of the guild. Paying a rhetor for his elder boy, and borrowing to do it.
      household: a wife, two sons, a slave-born clerk he freed last year; cares about his wife Prima and the two boys he is putting through a grammarian; wants to be the man two shops send for when they can't agree.
  Cassianus, a grain and oil merchant with warehouses under the Aventine. Suing Pollio before the praetor over the pump.
      household: a widowed sister who keeps his accounts, a daughter, porters; cares about his daughter's dowry; wants to keep his oil dry and his name good with the Ostia shippers.
  Diodoros, Cassianus's porter, freeborn, from Antioch. Running Cassianus's deliveries; his mother in Antioch has died.
      household: a room above a bakery with two other porters; cares about a mother in Antioch he sends money to; wants more responsibility and more pay than carrying jars.
  Titus Aelius Serenus, a physician of the Subura, a member of the Physicians' Circle. In a public quarrel with a fashionable physician who treats senators.
      household: his sister, a freedman who grinds his drugs, and a cat that sleeps on the casebooks; cares about a sister in the Subura who keeps his house and his casebooks; wants to show that a physician can know which treatment works, not just argue for one.
  Gaius Fabius Crispus, a bronze worker with a shop on the Clivus, the one who first cut seats to your foot. Running one powered shop and refusing to open a second.
      household: Livia, two daughters, his nephew Marcus at the bench, an old slave who keeps the furnace; cares about Livia, their two daughters, and his sister's boy Marcus; wants for his shop to be the one other shops copy, not the one that copies.
  Livia, Gaius's wife, who runs the household and half the shop's accounts. Arranging her elder daughter's betrothal to a Green-supporting baker's son.
      household: the same as Gaius's; cares about her daughters, and Gaius more than she lets him see; wants daughters well married and a shop that doesn't eat the family.
  Marcus Fabius Tertius, Gaius's sister's son, at the bench in Gaius's shop. An apprentice who thinks he should be a journeyman.
      household: his mother, in two rooms above a cookshop; cares about his widowed mother, and being taken seriously; wants a shop of his own, the one his father the clerk never got.
  Lucan, an old smith near the Porta Trigemina who taught half the smiths on the river. Dead; buried by his burial club on the Via Ostiensis. Laid up or away for now.
      household: a granddaughter and too many cats; cares about the craft, and a granddaughter who sells eggs; wants to see one of his apprentices do better than him.
  Aulus Septimius Crispus, a millwright who keeps the mills on the Janiculum turning. Laid up with his back; his younger son is keeping the mills.
      household: a wife who sells bread from the mill, two grown sons; cares about his sons, both millwrights at Ostia; wants mills that run all year without him being called out at night.
  Sextus Nerius, owner of a finishing shop where twelve men polish and fit bronze by hand. Proud and baffled: his son has argued his first case in the Basilica Julia.
      household: a wife, a son who wants to be a lawyer; cares about his men, some of whom have been with him twenty years; wants to keep his shop and his men, and to be paid what the work is worth.

== Civilization
  Active:
    Shared measures: reproducible
    Gauges and fits: reproducible
    True valve seats: reproducible, copied, badly
    Line shafts: reproducible
    Replaceable bearing blocks: reproducible
    Controlled transmission: reproducible
    Powered boring and grinding: prototype
    Medicine 54.2 (Stable)   (why medicine)
    Governance 55 (Stable)   (why governance)
    Economy 61.3 (Stable)   (why economy)
  Emerging:
    Dimensioned drawings: Drawings show the shape but not the sizes another shop would need.
    Graded tool steel: Hardness varies from batch to batch and nobody writes down why. Knowing is not making: graded tool steel needs recorded trials first.
    Rigid lathe work: Hand power and a cutter held by hand can't hold a size in hard metal. Knowing is not making: rigid lathe work needs graded tool steel first.
    Interchangeable parts: Metal parts are still fitted one by one. Knowing is not making: interchangeable parts needs dimensioned drawings and rigid lathe work first.
    The hydraulic press: Seals and cylinders give way long before the pressure a press needs. Knowing is not making: the hydraulic press needs recipes for bronze first.
    The governor: No one has made a machine that corrects its own speed.

> view journal
== Journal
  Available now:
    ledger   (ledger)
    news   (news)
  Archived:
    October, AD 165: Lucan dies in the winter, in his sleep, with a cat on his feet. His burial club, the smiths of the Porta Trigemina, pays for the pyre and a small stone. His granddaughter asks you to say something at the tomb. You say what he said to you.
    Aprilis, AD 164: Sextus's son argues his first case in the Basilica Julia, a dispute over a party wall. Sextus sits at the back in his best tunic and understands one word in five. The son loses. Sextus tells everyone on the Clivus he won.
    December, AD 163: Priscus offers to go halves with Gaius on a second powered shop by the river. Gaius says no. You argue the numbers with him all evening. "One shop I can watch," he says. "Two shops, I'm a man who walks between them." Livia agrees with him, which settles it.
    November, AD 163: Lucan's eyes have gone. He sits on a stool by the forge he gave to his best apprentice and listens to the hammering. "Too cold," he says, without looking. The apprentice puts the iron back in the fire.
    Augustus, AD 163: Aulus's back gives out lifting a stone that his sons told him to leave. He is on a board in his kitchen for a season, giving orders through the window. His younger son keeps the mills and does everything slightly differently, on purpose.
    Iulius, AD 162: Livia has settled her elder daughter's betrothal: a baker's son from the next street, a Green like her. Gaius wanted a bronze worker. Livia says a baker's son will always eat. The betrothal ring is iron, the custom, and the girl wears it on the fourth finger and looks at it all day.
    Augustus, AD 159: A letter comes from Antioch, months old: Diodoros's mother has died. He reads it in the street, folds it into his tunic and finishes the morning's deliveries. That evening he spends a week's pay on a funeral meal for porters who never met her.
    Maius, AD 159: Felix's elder boy has gone to a rhetor at the Forum of Trajan. The fees are more than Felix makes in a good month. He tells you about it with his arms folded, daring you to say it's too much. Prima has already said it.
    Martius, AD 159: Pollio, across the lane from Cassianus, has had a pump made like his by a bronzesmith on the Clivus who saw it once. The seats are cut by eye and it leaks back on every stroke. Pollio tells everyone the design is yours.
    November, AD 158: A fashionable physician who treats senators calls Serenus's casebooks 'the accounts of a grocer' at a public lecture. Serenus stands up in the audience and asks him how many of his patients died last summer. The Circle talks of nothing else for a month, and two senators' households stop calling Serenus at all.
    Augustus, AD 158: Diodoros can't carry with that hand, so Cassianus has him run the deliveries and see to the pump. He knows its seats better than Cassianus does.
    December, AD 157: Fire in the warehouses under the Aventine in the night. Cassianus loses a season's oil. His porter Diodoros burns his hand dragging jars out of the smoke. Below it all, in the wet cellar, your pump still draws.

> jump
You will leave AD 166 and arrive somewhere between AD 196 and AD 211: the machine's range is 30–45 years (repairs, 0 of 3 upgrades, and your time in this era). You can't come back.
What you leave behind (11,087 denarii and 9 aurei in hand):
  • Medicine debt 0.9 keeps growing 5% a year for 30 years after you leave (about 1.4 in a decade, 3.8 after 30 years) unless a crisis clears it. Paying it down now costs 66 denarii.
  • No last orders for the Guild (leading now: the Ostia Grain Cartel): orders guild freetraders|cartel.
  • The Merchants' Guild of Ostia would be left bare, losing 10% of its strength each decade (now 40).
  •   It holds no money, so it can't pay down Economy debt while you're away. (Only someone who controls it, with 50%, can endow it.)
  • The machine will carry you 30–45 years (repairs, 0 of 3 upgrades, and your time in this era); exactly how far, you'll know when you arrive. Upgrades and more time here would lengthen it.
  • The 11,087 denarii in your hands stay behind and are lost unless you spend them, pay down debt or endow an institution.
  • Your gold: the machine can carry 10 aurei; you hold 9 aurei.
  •   Denarii can't be carried, banked or buried usefully: change them into gold first (exchange <n> denarii).
Prepare: paydown <domain> <points> · endow <inst> <denarii|all> · audit <inst> · exchange <n> denarii · deposit <aurei> · bury <aurei>. Type 'jump' again to go, or anything else to stay.

> jump

The machine shudders. Decades pass in the dark... It carries you 40 years.

— Recognition —
You climb out of the machine in AD 206. The Tiber smells the same. Near the Porta Trigemina you hear
a single hammer: the smith's workshop you funded is still working, sooty and a little larger, with a
boy asleep by the bellows. Down the street, the fountain you didn't choose was filled in long ago. An
old woman calls the spot 'fever corner'.

— Wrongness —
Something is wrong. The cattle market has spilled over its walls; you hear six languages haggling
over bales from half the world. The coins are lighter and paler than you remember, barely half silver
now. Prices have climbed with every cut, and soldiers grumble that their pay buys less each year.

— Personal echo —
Demetria's letters survive in a library copy. They name everyone who stayed to help the sick in 166.
You are not among them; she never thought to ask you. In the finishers' quarter the men who run the
grinders are called Nerius's boys, though none of them ever worked for him. In the guild hall at
Ostia the old contracts on the wall have one witness over and over: Felix. The fitters call him Felix
who connected the shops. One says he once sponsored a foreign craftsman; no one remembers the name.
The warehouses under the Aventine were rebuilt in brick after a fire, long ago. The cellar under the
third one is still dry.

— Discovery —
The guild you backed is now the Ostia Grain Cartel. A handful of houses that decide who may land
grain at Ostia and at what price, and pay the magistrates to keep it so. They know who you are, and
they do not want you back. Rules cut to the shared foot are cheap now, cheaper than arguing over
measures. Builders' contracts on the Aventine name the foot by the founder's stamp. A fitter's gauges
cost less than a bad job. Shops that don't use them can't sell to the shippers, and say so bitterly.
A true seat costs less now than a day's bailing. Bakers and fullers who never owned a pump have one
in the yard, and the porters who used to bail have other work or none. Powered shops along the race
undersell the hand shops on the Clivus. The hand shops that are left do the fine work, or close.
Bearing shells are sold by the basket at Ostia, cheap enough that nobody waits for a worn one to
fail. In the powered shops a boy works a lever that lets a rope go slack, and one machine stops while
the others run on. You see the other kind, too: seats cut by eye to look like the good ones, leaking
back on every stroke. The men who sell them say the design is the foreigner's. In your purse: the 9
aurei you carried, as good as the day you left.

You can walk around Rome: visit market, changers, forges, curia or subura (no Attention).
Type 'learn more' for the Index and what became of your institutions, 'jump' to go on another 25–40 years, or 'quit'.

  AD 189: the pestilence returns to Rome under Commodus. At its worst two thousand die in a day; the emperor goes to Laurentum for the clean air. It is contained: about 32 thousand dead, 3% of Rome.

(after arrival) > jump

The machine shudders. Decades pass in the dark... It carries you 30 years.

— Recognition —
You climb out again in AD 236, 30 years after your last visit. The forge by the Porta Trigemina still
smokes; the family who run it now have never heard of a partner who drew machines on wax. 'Fever
corner' is still what they call the spot where the fountain was.

— Wrongness —
The markets have grown past anything you left: new warehouses along the river, and moneychangers who
quote rates for cities you've never heard of. The coins are worse again: grey, light, barely silver
at all. Prices are quoted in bundles of coins now, not single ones.

— Personal echo —
Demetria's list of those who stayed in 166 is carved on a wall now, in a portico by the Tiber. You
read it twice. You are not on it. The carters' yard by the Porta Trigemina has become three yards and
a feud. Diodoros's tombstone stands in the oldest one: a man with one hand raised, a citizen of
Antioch and of Rome, it says, though he was never a citizen of either. A physician quotes Serenus's
fevers at you to prove a point Serenus would have hated. The casebooks have been copied so often that
half the cases in them never happened.

— Discovery —
The Ostia Grain Cartel is much as you found it last time, only older: the same people in charge of
it, and fewer who remember why. Every fitter on the river works to one foot now, and the guild keeps
the bronze master in its hall. Fitters on the quays carry fit and no-fit plugs in a leather roll, and
buy seats from whichever shop is nearest. The pumps on the Ostia quays have seats cut to a gauge now.
The guild's fitters carry the gauges in a box, and a pump that leaks back is a joke. Every new shop
on the river is built around a shaft. Men speak of a shop's 'wheel' the way they speak of a ship's
hull. The cheap seats are still sold at the market gates, and still leak. People call them Pollio's
seats now, and say the good ones were stolen from him by a foreigner. In your purse: the 9 aurei you
carried, as good as the day you left.

You can walk around Rome: visit market, changers, forges, curia or subura (no Attention).
Type 'learn more' for the Index and what became of your institutions or 'quit'.
